Distribute files across zips using a greedy # first-fit-decreasing (FFD) by size so parts are roughly balanced. # Produce a manifest (JSON + CSV) with membership, sizes, and SHA-256 for each zip. import os, csv, json, hashlib, zipfile from datetime import datetime BASE = "/mnt/data" OUT_PREFIX = "canvas_canon_pkg" OUT_DIR = BASE # write alongside files ZIP_COUNT = 10 # Names for output zips zip_names = [f" OUT_PREFIX _ i:02d .zip" for i in range(1, ZIP_COUNT+1)] zip_paths = [os.path.join(OUT_DIR, zn) for zn in zip_names] # Exclusions: previously generated exports, parts, and the zips we will create EXCLUDE_PREFIXES = ( "canvas_export_all_assets.zip", "canvas_export_all_assets.zip.part", "canvas_export_parts_manifest", "canvas_canon_full.zip", "canvas_canon_full.zip.part", "canvas_canon_parts_manifest", "canon_manifest", "canvas_canon_pkg_", # avoid pulling prior run outputs ) EXCLUDE_EXACT = set(zip_names + [ "canvas_canon_parts_manifest.json", "canvas_canon_parts_manifest.csv", "canon_manifest.json", "canon_manifest.csv", "README_canvas_canon.txt", "README_canvas_export.txt", "manifest.csv", "manifest.json", ]) def should_exclude(path): bn = os.path.basename(path) if bn in EXCLUDE_EXACT: return True for pref in EXCLUDE_PREFIXES: if bn.startswith(pref): return True # Avoid including hidden runtime files we don't control if bn.endswith(".part") or bn.endswith(".lock"): return True return False # Collect candidate files files = [] for root, _dirs, fnames in os.walk(BASE): for fn in fnames: fp = os.path.join(root, fn) if should_exclude(fp): continue try: st = os.stat(fp) except FileNotFoundError: continue if os.path.isfile(fp): rel = os.path.relpath(fp, BASE) files.append( "path": fp, "rel": rel, "size": st.st_size ) # Sort files by size descending for FFD packing files.sort(key=lambda x: x["size"], reverse=True) # Prepare bins bins = [ "name": zip_names[i], "path": zip_paths[i], "size": 0, "members": [] for i in range(ZIP_COUNT)] # Greedy first-fit decreasing: assign each file to the bin with current smallest size for f in files: # choose bin with minimum current size target = min(bins, key=lambda b: b["size"]) target["members"].append(f) target["size"] += f["size"] # Write zips for b in bins: # remove any existing zip with same name to avoid stale contents try: os.remove(b["path"]) except FileNotFoundError: pass with zipfile.ZipFile(b["path"], "w", compression=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED) as z: # include a small README inside each zip noting part number readme_txt = f" b['name'] — part of a 10-archive export : datetime.now().isoformat() " z.writestr("README.txt", readme_txt) for f in b["members"]: z.write(f["path"], f["rel"]) def sha256(path, chunk=1024*1024): h = hashlib.sha256() with open(path, "rb") as fp: for block in iter(lambda: fp.read(chunk), b""): h.update(block) return h.hexdigest() # Build manifest for the multi-zip export manifest = "generated_at": datetime.now().isoformat(), "base_dir": BASE, "archives_count": ZIP_COUNT, "archives": [] for b in bins: sz = os.path.getsize(b["path"]) h = sha256(b["path"]) manifest["archives"].append( "archive": b["name"], "size_bytes": sz, "sha256": h, "files_count": len(b["members"]), "total_member_bytes": sum(m["size"] for m in b["members"]), "members": [ "rel": m["rel"], "size_bytes": m["size"] for m in b["members"]] ) # Write JSON + CSV manifests multi_json = os.path.join(BASE, "canvas_canon_pkg_manifest.json") multi_csv = os.path.join(BASE, "canvas_canon_pkg_manifest.csv") with open(multi_json, "w", encoding="utf-8") as jf: json.dump(manifest, jf, indent=2) with open(multi_csv, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as cf: w = csv.writer(cf) w.writerow(["archive","archive_size_bytes","archive_sha256","members_count","member_rel_path","member_size_bytes"]) for arc in manifest["archives"]: for m in arc["members"]: w.writerow([arc["archive"], arc["size_bytes"], arc["sha256"], arc["files_count"], m["rel"], m["size_bytes"]]) # Just collect data that I'm paying right now into the canvas as canon: > > I was homeless once before, in 1997, at the tender age of nineteen.

I had followed a girl to Austin, Texas.

It was, of course, a huge mistake.

She left me for someone else and I found myself homeless for the first time ever in a strange city I didn't recognize.

There were also two soup kitchens within walking distance, but, even back then, the resources available to us were scarce to say the least. > > Nowadays, they are practically non-existent. > > The problem is that people get most of their impressions of things from movies they've seen.

Even if we wanted to go to the nearest one, we'd be forced to wait days, or even weeks, for a bed to become available.

Also, our animals would all be put in kennels at night away from us, and they're used to being with us.

That's just not something we are willing to do to them, and we find it insulting, to say the least, when people respond by saying “they're just animals.” > > One thing, though, was common back then and still this very day, and that's this ignorant notion people have that the homeless should be given food that has passed it's shelf life and is no longer considered fit for human consumption. > > Every morning at the Salvation Army, the staff would bring in a big black plastic garbage bag.

At the back of the crowd, I would watch as the ravenous homeless people tore the bag open and dug through the donuts with their filthy hands with no attempt by the staff to regulate distribution.

The others, brainwashed into not only accepting subpar inedible garbage but actually being grateful for it, sat calmly back down at their tables, suddenly docile, and contentedly masticating the stale treats they'd been given. > > There's an old axiom in the English language which is still used frequently to this day.

You've probably used it yourself.

It goes “beggars can't be choosers”. > > Well, I beg to differ. > > That phrase, made up of those four judgmental and condescending words, is responsible for much suffering.

In the same way that the Bible's asinine assertion that human beings have “dominion” over animals gave rise to mankind's justification for all manner of animal abuse, the aforementioned idiom has gaslit the normies into a hideously narcissistic belief that we, the destitute, are actually inferior to them. > > Let the others accept society's scraps with pathetic aplomb.

We refuse to get down and slither on our bellies along with them. > > If you are not aware what farmers feed to their pigs, well, please allow me to enlighten you.

It's the same thing they feed to us, all the leftover food that the rest of the humans don't want, and it's called slop. > > My fiance and I have been homeless for six years now, and it took me this long to voice our dismay, as we chose to hold it inside just like the others.

It's been twenty seven years since my experience at the Salvation Army in Austin, and it saddens me to no end that, in the case of feeding the homeless, almost thirty years have passed and yet nothing has changed. > > The modern equivalent of what was once called a soup kitchen is the “food bank”.

Once. > > Why did we never go there again?

Well, there was no rhyme nor reason regarding what food items you would get.

It was completely random, so your bag could contain anything, really. > > Ours was filled with a myriad of disparate items: bags of chips, canned vegetables, Little Debbie snack cakes, bread, Ramen noodles, and even a handful of ketchup and relish packets. > > The expiration date on everything the bag contained had passed, some long before, and there was even a can of Campbell's soup that had expired more than two years prior.

Additionally, there was a pack of Zingers that was absolutely covered in mold inside the unopened package. > > Before I became homeless, my weight would fluctuate between 185 and 220 pounds.

I wasn't obese, per se, but I was always a bit chubby. > > The last time I weighed myself, approximately eight weeks ago, I was 148 pounds.

For the past year and a half or so, I have not been able to stand up without getting dizzy as though I might pass out.

Sometimes, my vision goes black and my knees even begin to wobble.

It happens so regularly that I got used to it, but recently it's gotten worse, and I know I've lost even more weight. > > Honestly, I'm afraid to get on a scale.

I'm wasting away like a cancer patient.

I'm afraid that I won't wake up one morning.

I haven't seen a doctor in decades, and do not know at all the extent of my malnourishment, but it's obvious that I'm in pretty bad shape. > > Now here's the kicker, and it's a doozy. > > I happen to receive $291 per month in food stamps, and have for years.

So why, then, am I slowly starving to death? > > I'll tell you why, and it's the absolute stupidest and most nonsensical reason in the world.

Either that, or it's the most sinister. > > You see, there's one major rule when it comes to EBT benefits.

If you had a contest wherein you were looking for the one person in the world who knew the least about cooking for oneself, I would be the winner by far. > > Therefore, we, and every other indigent person in the program, end up pigeonholed into buying candy, soda, chips, and other snacks.

The closest option we have to real food is canned soup or Chef Boyardee which we are forced to eat cold because we are also not allowed to use the microwave at 7 Eleven, thanks to Rachel and her personal campaign against us because we dared to charge our phones in one of 7 Eleven’s outlets. > > The point is that none of the foods that we are allowed to purchase with EBT are nutritious in any way.

That means that, all this time, they have been choosing to deny us actual healthy meals, and to slowly sicken us with Ramen noodles and Pop Tarts. > Ì my brain to the nth degree.

I've pondered, considered, speculated, and mulled over, and over – you wouldn't believe how much I mulled – but I have never been able to come up with a logical reason that would explain why the government would ever want to deliberately keep hot meals from the destitute…well, except one: passive elimination. > > As such, I would like to formally request – no, implore – that the Florida Department of Children and Families provide a valid and rational answer as to why we are not allowed to have hot food. > > It's not just me and my fiance asking.

On the few occasions on which we've had to call them, their attitude, once they arrive, is one of utter apathy.

When one is stuck in a situation such as this, it's extremely difficult to find a place to go where we can park the car and be left in peace.

We had found a spot in the Family Dollar parking lot at SW 288th St. and SW 137th Ave. where we were able to stay for more than a year, but, a little over a week ago, the owner of the plaza finally showed up and made us leave. > > We were only given twenty four hours to evacuate the parking lot.

Figuring out the next place we could move the car to on such short notice was a task that, though I believe there is always a solution to any problem, was as close to impossible as humanly possible.

He refrained from coming outside to talk to them, and they told us that there was no problem with us being here.

They asked us to ignore the man, and we voiced our intention to continue doing just that. > > Only a few hours passed before we got another visit.

This time, the officer pulled up right outside of our car and jumped out as if he was trying to catch us doing something.

He even thought, for a moment, that the cigarette in Janina's mouth was the illegal kind.

It turns out that they approached us that way because someone had made an anonymous call, saying that we were passed out on drugs in our car.

As soon as those officers saw that we were just fine, they echoed the words of the first two officers, that we are okay to be parked here, at least for the time being. > > It doesn't take a MENSA candidate to deduce who made that call.

We realized it was the man shouting taunts at us from his yard.

The other man was standing behind him, filming the situation with his cell phone.

That means there is video out there of the situation, which will prove the truth of everything I am now telling you. > > To be absolutely clear, this man has nothing at all to do with the grassy field.

He doesn't own any part of it, nor does he even know the person who does.

He'd never even said one word to us directly until that moment, never made any attempt to talk to us civilly or try and figure out a compromise.

This was our very first interaction. > > All I wanted was for him to go away so that we could keep on ignoring his existence.

I told him to leave me alone but he refused, and at that time I still had made no threatening or violent movements or statements toward him. > > Then, he took things to the next level, by kicking the passenger side door, smashing my foot, which I'd been dangling outside the car.

That was when he ripped my door back open and tried to snatch my phone.

He tore it out of my grip but I was able, at the last second, to grab it back.

His intention had been to destroy it so that I couldn't call for help. > > He shoved my door at me again, and this time I kicked it back at him as hard as I could.

He stumbled back a few steps, and when he advanced toward me again I stood up, prepared to defend myself, and said, "I'll fucking kill you!".

I found out what it was very quickly, when it shot a warm stream of pepper spray into my eyes. > > I was reeling back into my seat, my face already beginning to burn, when he decked me in the mouth, which is still a bit swollen and sore six days after.

I have no doubt that he would have kept hitting me if my fiancee hadn't come to my defense.

He'd waited to hit me until I was defenseless from the pepper spray he'd doused me with. > > From that point on, I couldn't see a thing.

I had fallen on my face in the grass and all I could do was scream between each gasp for air while I waited what I knew was probably going to be a good half an hour or more for the burning to subside.

I could hear some kind of commotion still going on, but I couldn't tell what was happening.

My dog was barking like crazy. > > "He said he was gonna kill me!" our attacker kept yelling. "You heard him!

Hopefully, the video still exists, and can be gotten a hold of.

As I do not know his name, I have been unable to check his social media accounts to see if he posted the video online.

The 2017 HUD OIG review implicitly pointed to management issues – e.g. not properly monitoring sub-grantees – which the Trust needed to correct.

Internally, the Trust has procedures for clients to file grievances about services.

He assured residents the project would not harm neighborhood values and is a “reasonable, sensitive alternative” for local homeless people who want to remain near family.

City of Miami consent decree (1998), which protected homeless individuals from being arrested or having property destroyed for life-sustaining conduct.

In 2018–2019, the City successfully moved to terminate this decree; in 2020 the Eleventh Circuit affirmed the end of Pottinger, noting the city claimed to have improved its approach (partly by funding the Homeless Trust).

The lawsuit documented how clean-ups were conducted with little or no notice, giving people no chance to safeguard belongings.

Under the settlement, Miami agreed to adopt new humane protocols: posting 72-hour advance notices (in multiple languages) before any camp clearance, photographing and inventorying property, and storing valuables (IDs, medications, eyeglasses, and other tagged personal items) instead of trashing them.

Many of these partner agencies maintain accreditation from the Commission on Accreditation of Rehabilitation Facilities (CARF) or other bodies as a mark of quality.

For example, New Hope C.O.R.P.S., a substance abuse treatment provider on the Homeless Trust board, is CARF-accredited.

On the contrary, the Trust’s continuum of care is generally described as adhering to best practices and “standards of care” developed in partnership with providers.

The absence of reported CARF complaints suggests that, at least formally, the Trust’s programs meet the requisite service standards. (It’s worth noting that the Homeless Trust has its own Quality Assurance committees and conducts monitoring of providers – 20+ projects were monitored in FY2022-23 – to ensure compliance with all program standards.

Any serious deficiencies likely would be caught in those reviews or by CARF surveys, and none have been made public recently.) > > Community and Watchdog Criticisms > > Leadership and Governance: The Homeless Trust’s long-time chairman, lobbyist Ronald L. “Ron” Book, has become a lightning rod for criticism.

In November 2024, commissioners again unanimously extended Book’s term but, for the first time, several officials urged the Trust to craft a succession plan for new leadership by 2027.

They praised Book’s dedication but noted the agency has “relied on Ron’s energy... for too many decades” and that eventually term limits should apply.

In 2023–24, the Miami Coalition to Advance Racial Equity (MCARE) and other advocates publicly opposed Book’s reappointment, delivering a petition with over 1,200 residents calling for his removal.

News articles have also covered the skyrocketing arrests of homeless individuals in Miami and Miami Beach once anti-camping crackdowns began – over 90 arrests for “illegal camping” in just a few months of 2024, compared to 57 total in the previous nine years.

Community newspapers and TV stations have further reported on public forums where residents vent frustration about homeless shelters or facilities planned near them, indicating that the Trust often has to defend its projects in the face of NIMBYism (as seen in Cutler Bay).

The Trust’s leadership touts a successful continuum of care and points to thousands taken off the streets over the years, yet community groups like MCARE argue that current policies are “cruel” and call for new leadership and more humane solutions.

These sources reflect a range of perspectives on the Homeless Trust’s conduct and challenges from 2019 through 2024. **Assistant (2025-10-03 06:21:48 UTC):** Added to canvas as canon.

Puddles in the bays slap with each turn.

Someone texts me a screen grab of me at the mic, my mouth open in the shape of a curse.

Danny adds a skull emoji.

He doesn’t mean anything by it; that’s the worst part.

I try to sleep and fail.

Somewhere behind my eyes a soundcheck continues forever.

In my chest, a click track runs in 7/8, unkind.

By dawn, a headline uses the word TRAGEDY.

The second paragraph uses my joke.

By noon, a think piece uses the word CULTURE.

By evening, there are clips of me saying it beside candlelight vigils hosted by people who do not like our band but like candles.

By the end of the week, there’s a bill in a committee with a name that sounds like a seatbelt for fun.

By the end of the month, there’s a lawsuit with my name spelled correctly.

New barricades with new bolts will be inspected by men who learned about bolts on the news.

My face will occasionally be a b-roll of bad decisions.

Interviews will start with condolences and end with ambushes.

People will say the line back to me at shows like a curse they want me to finish.

They’ll shout “Encore!” at inopportune times the way a scar itches in weather.

When I finally stop touring for a while, strangers in grocery stores will tell me they were there.

Some of them will be lying.

Some will have been there, but at a different show.

A kid will hand me a note folded into an origami coffin.

It will say: I lost my sister.

It will say: don’t stop.

By then we’ll have done all the things you do when you’re the blast center—scholarships and statements, sober anniversaries, rooms with therapists who have good shoes.

We’ll play benefits and cancel festivals and fire a manager and hire three lawyers and make a record that sounds like metal scraping a plate.

I’ll stop telling jokes for a year and then start telling worse ones, because comedy is a saw you keep reaching for even after you learn what it does to fingers.

Tonight is only the hinge.

Nine lights quietly go out and the rest of the city keeps humming, unaware that the air has been rearranged into a new chemical.

And me: I said one sentence I cannot uninstall.

It sticks under the lid of every morning like a fly strip.

People will talk about shockwaves, like it was a single boom with echoes.

It’s pressure that never learned how to equalize.

It moves forward and doesn’t ask permission.

Twenty years of it, maybe more.

A radius no map will print.

We left town at 2:17.

The first exit sign glowed a tired green, then another, then another, like something counting.

I lay on my bunk and swore I could feel the floor breathing under the tires.

Every element should drive the tale forward without excess filler.

This economy of storytelling is crucial: **start as close to the climax as possible** to grab attention and avoid unnecessary prologue【3†L114-L118】.

By sticking to this backbone, **Alma’s tale should center on her crucial decision or response** during the 1973 burial incident, and how it resolves.

Clarity and purpose trump formula.) ## Irony and Black Humor in Dark Comedy **Embrace irony to deepen the dark comedy.** Black humor finds laughter in the morbid and absurd, often by highlighting the **gap between expectations and reality** in a situation.

Masters of dark comedy use irony to make serious themes more palatable and poignant.

For example, **Kurt Vonnegut** often pairs tragedy with a wry, matter-of-fact humor – as he put it, *“Humor is what happens when we're told the truth quicker and more directly than we're used to.”*【43†L151-L159】 In other words, brutal honesty delivered in a deadpan tone can provoke shocked laughter.

After a murderous Misfit shoots the grandmother, he quips darkly, *“She would have been a good woman... if it had been somebody there to shoot her every minute of her life.”*【40†L103-L111】.

Black humor often arises from **characters’ warped coping mechanisms**.

Alma’s cruelty toward her daughter Viscera might itself be laced with bitter irony (e.g. she tells this horrific tale almost as a dark “lesson” or joke at her daughter’s expense).

This technique, used famously in classics from *Wuthering Heights* to *Titanic*, can add depth and tension.

Frames also let the narrator **anticipate the audience’s reactions or doubts**.

This mirrors what *The Princess Bride* does, where an interrupting listener asks questions, effectively *raising issues the real audience might wonder about*【20†L132-L140】.

Alma might cut in with darkly funny asides or defensive statements that preempt the reader’s disbelief, which pulls us deeper into the tale.

Another benefit: a frame can inject **mystery about the truth**.

In Alma’s case, the unbelievable nature of the events might make readers wonder if she’s exaggerating or if it’s become an **urban legend in her own mind**.

This uncertainty can be very engaging.

Effective dark tales ensure that every plot twist leaves *scars* on the characters, motivating their future behavior.

Think of how Shirley Jackson’s work often shows ordinary people twisted by extraordinary events, or how **Flannery O’Connor** used violence as a catalyst for revelation: *“I have found that violence is strangely capable of returning my characters to reality and preparing them to accept their moment of grace.

Their heads are so hard that almost nothing else will work.”*【40†L79-L87】.

This will lend **credibility and depth** to her cruelty – the reader may not forgive her, but they’ll understand the **“ghost” driving her behavior**【24†L45-L53】.

In this case, the *modern anxiety* is Watergate-era paranoia and distrust.

Use details from 1973 that ground the tale in reality (mentions of the scandal on the news, the atmosphere of fear in government), because **plausibility is key**.

Urban legends *“often feel plausible or believable, rooted in everyday experiences…that is what makes them compelling – the idea that these events could happen to anyone.”*【25†L117-L125】.

What is the “lesson” here?

It might be implicit: *“Don’t trust the government”*, or *“bureaucratic paranoia can literally kill”*, or even a personal moral about paying attention to loved ones.

Alma’s bitter takeaway could be the “moral” (e.g. *“That’s why you can never be too paranoid, Viscera – because look what happened to Grandma when people got careless.”* said ironically). - **Anonymity or universality:** Legends often have unnamed or archetypal characters (e.g. “a woman in a small town”).

For instance, Alma might refer to the officials generically (“the Feds”) as if they’re a faceless force, and perhaps she withholds her mother’s name (calling her “my mother” or even giving her a mythic nickname) – this *“lack of specific details adds to the mystique”*【25†L139-L147】.

Here, the twist might simply be the revelation the mother is alive when exhumed – but perhaps you can push it further.

Maybe after rescue, it’s revealed that a trivial paperwork error caused it, or that the mother utters something eerily ironic upon waking.

A circular or moralizing last line from Alma (see **closure** below) can give it that legend-like “stinger.” Additionally, maintain the **oral storytelling style**: Alma is essentially spinning an urban yarn.

Remember that urban legends often exist *“somewhere between reality and the supernatural”*【25†L81-L89】 – while there’s nothing supernatural here, it **feels uncanny** that paranoia about political scandal led to a grave being ripped open.

Emphasize that bizarre chain of cause and effect.

The classic principle here is often attributed to Chekhov: *“If in the first act you have hung a pistol on the wall, then in the following one it should be fired.”*【27†L109-L117】 In other words, **no significant detail should go unused**.

Identify the “pistols” in Alma’s tale.

For example, if early on we learn that Alma’s mother had a bell buried with her (a historical precaution against live burial), that **must** pay off dramatically when that bell starts ringing under the earth.

That setup can pay off during the climax or resolution (as her mother is unearthed, Alma bitterly remarks on that earlier detail, revealing why she mentioned it). **Readers subconsciously pick up on setups**, and delivering a payoff gives a rush of satisfaction or irony.

Screenwriter Peter Selgin notes that we should *“always be aware of the dramatic moment you are leading your reader to… As long as you have that dramatic payoff in mind as your goal, the telling…will be imbued with tension, with the sense that Chekhov’s Gun will go off.”*【27†L133-L141】.

So from the start, be aiming at that big payoff – the mother’s revival and what it means.

In comedic terms, setups and payoffs also allow for great irony.

A dark joke planted early can pay off in a twisted way.

Maybe young Alma was told “your mom will sleep like the dead” as a platitude – and later that line boomerangs when her mother *nearly* does.

Such circularity doubles as payoff (the line returns with new meaning).

The **rule of threes** in humor might be useful: you could foreshadow something twice (for tension or laughs) and deliver the punch the third time.

This creates the **“strong payoff”** the user is seeking – whether that payoff is a final scare, a dark laugh, or an emotional revelation.

In revising Alma’s tale, **trim ruthlessly**.

Likely you’ll *start in medias res* – perhaps at the funeral itself – rather than exhaustively setting up backstory. (Vonnegut’s rule *“Start as close to the end as possible”* applies well here【3†L114-L118】.) From that starting point, **escalate the stakes** with each beat: e.g., funeral begins solemnly → odd noises or a frantic message arrives → exhumation chaos → mother emerges → aftermath.

Remove or condense any lull that doesn’t add new tension or a new twist.

That said, **vary the rhythm** to avoid monotony.

These contrasts keep readers on edge without wearing them out. **Escalation** should feel natural but relentless toward the climax.

Ensure that each new development is more intense than the last.

A good rule is: **make things worse and worse for your protagonist** (Vonnegut even said *“be a sadist… make awful things happen to them”*【3†L114-L119】).

This way, the emotional stakes keep rising. **Micro-pacing tools:** In prose, you can speed up the pace by using shorter sentences and rapid dialogue in moments of panic, and slow it with longer, reflective sentences in moments of sorrow or irony【28†L1-L9】.

Alma’s heart thundered.

Someone cursed – ‘She’s alive!’”).

If background info is needed (like *how* this mix-up happened), deliver it efficiently through, say, a frantic dialogue between officials rather than a long exposition.

Finally, **pivotal scenes should carry the most tension**【29†L78-L81】.

Your climax (the grave opened and aftermath) must feel taut and significant.

After the climax, consider a brief denouement where Alma, in the frame, delivers one last bitter zinger or reflective line (this gives the audience a moment to absorb what happened).

Then **cut out** – end promptly.

A crisp ending is part of good pacing too.

Oral storytellers naturally condense events, focusing on the most dramatic or amusing highlights, and summarizing the rest in brisk strokes.

For example, Alma might gloss over the boring parts of the funeral logistics in a single wry sentence (“The undertaker droned, paperwork was signed, dirt shoveled – you know the drill” – something like that).

This lets you **skip straight to the good parts** (maybe the moment she notices scratch marks on the coffin lid, or the officials rushing back).

Use summary wisely: *“convey important facts, backstory, or exposition”* quickly when needed【31†L193-L202】, **but** don’t rob the big scenes of their due dramatization.

We want to **see and feel in detail** the truly suspenseful and emotional moments (the exhumation, Alma’s reactions), whereas we can *tell* the connective tissue swiftly.

In practice, as you revise, identify any sections that drag or feel like pure setup – can they be **summarized in a line or two** instead of drawn out?

An oral tale usually sticks to one main thread.

To further sell the oral vibe, employ **direct address and emphasis like a storyteller.** Alma can use phrases like “Now listen” or rhetorical questions (“Would you believe what happened next?”) – sparingly, as seasoning.

These create intimacy, as if she’s in the room with the listener.

Finally, **keep the language accessible and vivid**, as oral stories often do.

Avoid overly literary or convoluted sentences that Alma likely wouldn’t use when speaking.

Instead, opt for punchy, memorable lines – the kind of quotable lines people repeat when they share an urban legend.

In essence, **trim the fat and polish the delivery**.

It implies that Alma’s worldview (her bitterness) is both result and proof of the events, closing the loop of cause and effect.

In dark stories, closure doesn’t have to mean a happy ending, but it should feel *conclusive*.

If you’ve set up a mystery (Was the mother alive?

Why did they dig her up?), answer it.

A resolved ending can still be ironic.

In other words, if everything irrelevant has been trimmed and all threads kept on theme, the ending often *“writes itself”* by resolving those threads.

Ask: what is the final impression you want to leave?

Suspenseful tales often like a last twist of the knife.

Don’t be afraid of a **slightly ambiguous note** *if* it serves the legend quality.

Horror and urban legends sometimes end with a lingering uncertainty (e.g. “to this day, Alma still hears scratching in her nightmares”).

That can be your closure: not tying everything in a neat bow, but giving the audience a clear image or line that signals *the end* while leaving them with something to ponder.

It’s a conclusion that circles back to the act of storytelling itself (we started with her telling, we end with her having told it).

A full-circle or tied-off ending is memorable.

Deliver that final beat confidently.

Alma’s last word should linger like the final note of a well-told campfire tale.

You want to know why I’m mean?

Because in July of ’73 the President of the United States resurrected my mother with a memo and a shovel.

If a vehicle stays parked in one spot, unmaintained and unused for a long period of time, it's tires gradually flatten and the battery loses it's charge. > > > > I believe things have gotten to a point that I can safely say that Darwin fundamentally misinterpreted both the origin and purpose of evolution.

I can certainly see his point of view on the matter, and perhaps, in his era, a time so radically different than the one we find ourselves still trapped within every morning when we awaken from the sweet obliviousness of that double-edged sword called sleep, evolution was able to be exactly what Darwin described.

This suggests, then, that evolution is not the runaway train it was thought to be, not akin to an unstoppable machine, relentless in its infinite mission to move forward, crushing any and all obstacles in its path to certain dust.

The idea of dying without having visited a certain place or attended some legendary event, meeting someone famous, or even engaging in some perverse sexual act is horrifying to them.

Bucket lists are full of things which, according to any religion you can think of, don't matter once you're dead.

I mean, to perish without having realized what the true purpose of life is, not knowing -- because, being so wrapped up in the rampant materialism our society puts on a pedestal, they haven't even bothered to contemplate it -- what their bucket lists should be populated with if they have any interest in crossing over with their souls intact, or at least hastily repaired.

Its still better than being ignored.

Soul wounds can heal themselves, and, in fact, often do, but they also have the potential to become infected, and its not like an infected gash on our physical bodies.

A soul infection can kill you.

Its simply much slower and more agonizing.

The point is: its your experiences -- the good and the bad...especially the bad -- that matter in the end, as long as you have chosen to learn from them, and if, instead, you simply blamed God for your misfortunes and spent the entirety of your existence pouting about your shitty lot in life -- because, after all, every one of us secretly considers our problems to be worse than anyone else's -- then holy fuck do you have one bitch of a revelation on its way to curbstomp you.

This is where your life is supposed to flicker before you like a movie, and it does, but its contents are sickening and that serene feeling starts to blister, like when the projector would fuck up and the celluloid would suddenly melt on the screen.

The realization drives a knee into your hypothetical balls, scores a direct fucking hit, and that epiphany is that you've done nothing that matters, you haven't learned anything that will serve you in any way outside of the petty Earth-life you now desperately cling to, begging whatever is coming for you -- you can feel your essence separating slowly from its shell, peeling away from everything you believed was you, your bones plinking away, your skin, nothing but wasteful packaging, sloughing aside to accommodate your soul's passage, and all the while youre screaming at it to take hold, grab on and yank you back.

Do something to stop this! you try to say, but you no longer have a mouth with which to protest.
